    Methods Documentation

    all\_pix2world(*\*args*, *\*\*kwargs*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.all_pix2world)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_pix2world "Link to this definition")
    :   Transforms pixel coordinates to world coordinates.

        Performs all of the following in series:

        > * Detector to image plane correction (if present in the
        >   FITS file)
        > * [SIP](https://irsa.ipac.caltech.edu/data/SPITZER/docs/files/spitzer/shupeADASS.pdf) distortion correction (if present in the FITS
        >   file)
        > * [distortion paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table-lookup correction (if present
        >   in the FITS file)
        > * [wcslib](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/wcslib/index.html) “core” WCS transformation

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *naxis* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

                For a transformation that is not two-dimensional, the
                two-argument form must be used.

            **ra\_dec\_order**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   When [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)") will ensure that world coordinates are always given
                and returned in as (*ra*, *dec*) pairs, regardless of the order of
                the axes specified by the in the `CTYPE` keywords. Default is
                [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)").

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the sky coordinates, in degrees. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`SingularMatrixError`](astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError.html#astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError "astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError")
            :   Linear transformation matrix is singular.

            [`InconsistentAxisTypesError`](astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError.html#astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError "astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError")
            :   Inconsistent or unrecognized coordinate axis types.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid parameter value.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   x- and y-coordinate arrays are not the same size.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Ill-conditioned coordinate transformation parameters.

        Notes

        The order of the axes for the result is determined by the
        `CTYPEia` keywords in the FITS header, therefore it may not
        always be of the form (*ra*, *dec*). The
        [`lat`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat"), [`lng`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng"),
        [`lattyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp") and [`lngtyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp")
        members can be used to determine the order of the axes.

    all\_world2pix(*\*arg*, *tolerance=1.0e-4*, *maxiter=20*, *adaptive=False*, *detect\_divergence=True*, *quiet=False*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.all_world2pix)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "Link to this definition")
    :   Transforms world coordinates to pixel coordinates, using
        numerical iteration to invert the full forward transformation
        [`all_pix2world`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_pix2world "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_pix2world") with complete
        distortion model.

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *naxis* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

                For a transformation that is not two-dimensional, the
                two-argument form must be used.

            **ra\_dec\_order**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   When [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)") will ensure that world coordinates are always given
                and returned in as (*ra*, *dec*) pairs, regardless of the order of
                the axes specified by the in the `CTYPE` keywords. Default is
                [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)").

            **tolerance**[`float`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#float "(in Python v3.15)"), optional (default = 1.0e-4)
            :   Tolerance of solution. Iteration terminates when the
                iterative solver estimates that the “true solution” is
                within this many pixels current estimate, more
                specifically, when the correction to the solution found
                during the previous iteration is smaller
                (in the sense of the L2 norm) than `tolerance`.

            **maxiter**[`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)"), optional (default = 20)
            :   Maximum number of iterations allowed to reach a solution.

            **quiet**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional (default = [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"))
            :   Do not throw [`NoConvergence`](astropy.wcs.NoConvergence.html#astropy.wcs.NoConvergence "astropy.wcs.NoConvergence") exceptions when
                the method does not converge to a solution with the
                required accuracy within a specified number of maximum
                iterations set by `maxiter` parameter. Instead,
                simply return the found solution.

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the pixel coordinates. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Other Parameters:
        :   **adaptive**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional (default = [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"))
            :   Specifies whether to adaptively select only points that
                did not converge to a solution within the required
                accuracy for the next iteration. Default is recommended
                for HST as well as most other instruments.

                Note

                The [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") uses a vectorized
                implementation of the method of consecutive
                approximations (see `Notes` section below) in which it
                iterates over *all* input points *regardless* until
                the required accuracy has been reached for *all* input
                points. In some cases it may be possible that
                *almost all* points have reached the required accuracy
                but there are only a few of input data points for
                which additional iterations may be needed (this
                depends mostly on the characteristics of the geometric
                distortions for a given instrument). In this situation
                it may be advantageous to set `adaptive` = [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)") in
                which case [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") will continue
                iterating *only* over the points that have not yet
                converged to the required accuracy. However, for the
                HST’s ACS/WFC detector, which has the strongest
                distortions of all HST instruments, testing has
                shown that enabling this option would lead to a about
                50-100% penalty in computational time (depending on
                specifics of the image, geometric distortions, and
                number of input points to be converted). Therefore,
                for HST and possibly instruments, it is recommended
                to set `adaptive` = [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"). The only danger in
                getting this setting wrong will be a performance
                penalty.

                Note

                When `detect_divergence` is [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"),
                [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") will automatically switch
                to the adaptive algorithm once divergence has been
                detected.

            **detect\_divergence**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional (default = [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"))
            :   Specifies whether to perform a more detailed analysis
                of the convergence to a solution. Normally
                [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") may not achieve the required
                accuracy if either the `tolerance` or `maxiter` arguments
                are too low. However, it may happen that for some
                geometric distortions the conditions of convergence for
                the method of consecutive approximations used by
                [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") may not be satisfied, in which
                case consecutive approximations to the solution will
                diverge regardless of the `tolerance` or `maxiter`
                settings.

                When `detect_divergence` is [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"), these divergent
                points will be detected as not having achieved the
                required accuracy (without further details). In addition,
                if `adaptive` is [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)") then the algorithm will not
                know that the solution (for specific points) is diverging
                and will continue iterating and trying to “improve”
                diverging solutions. This may result in `NaN` or
                `Inf` values in the return results (in addition to a
                performance penalties). Even when `detect_divergence`
                is [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"), [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix"), at the end of the
                iterative process, will identify invalid results
                (`NaN` or `Inf`) as “diverging” solutions and will
                raise [`NoConvergence`](astropy.wcs.NoConvergence.html#astropy.wcs.NoConvergence "astropy.wcs.NoConvergence") unless the `quiet`
                parameter is set to [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)").

                When `detect_divergence` is [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"),
                [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") will detect points for which
                current correction to the coordinates is larger than
                the correction applied during the previous iteration
                **if** the requested accuracy **has not yet been
                achieved**. In this case, if `adaptive` is [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"),
                these points will be excluded from further iterations and
                if `adaptive` is [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"), [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") will
                automatically switch to the adaptive algorithm. Thus, the
                reported divergent solution will be the latest converging
                solution computed immediately *before* divergence
                has been detected.

                Note

                When accuracy has been achieved, small increases in
                current corrections may be possible due to rounding
                errors (when `adaptive` is [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)")) and such
                increases will be ignored.

                Note

                Based on our testing using HST ACS/WFC images, setting
                `detect_divergence` to [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)") will incur about 5-20%
                performance penalty with the larger penalty
                corresponding to `adaptive` set to [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)").
                Because the benefits of enabling this
                feature outweigh the small performance penalty,
                especially when `adaptive` = [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"), it is
                recommended to set `detect_divergence` to [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"),
                unless extensive testing of the distortion models for
                images from specific instruments show a good stability
                of the numerical method for a wide range of
                coordinates (even outside the image itself).

                Note

                Indices of the diverging inverse solutions will be
                reported in the `divergent` attribute of the
                raised [`NoConvergence`](astropy.wcs.NoConvergence.html#astropy.wcs.NoConvergence "astropy.wcs.NoConvergence") exception object.

        Raises:
        :   [`NoConvergence`](astropy.wcs.NoConvergence.html#astropy.wcs.NoConvergence "astropy.wcs.NoConvergence")
            :   The method did not converge to a
                solution to the required accuracy within a specified
                number of maximum iterations set by the `maxiter`
                parameter. To turn off this exception, set `quiet` to
                [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"). Indices of the points for which the requested
                accuracy was not achieved (if any) will be listed in the
                `slow_conv` attribute of the
                raised [`NoConvergence`](astropy.wcs.NoConvergence.html#astropy.wcs.NoConvergence "astropy.wcs.NoConvergence") exception object.

                See [`NoConvergence`](astropy.wcs.NoConvergence.html#astropy.wcs.NoConvergence "astropy.wcs.NoConvergence") documentation for
                more details.

            [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`SingularMatrixError`](astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError.html#astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError "astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError")
            :   Linear transformation matrix is singular.

            [`InconsistentAxisTypesError`](astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError.html#astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError "astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError")
            :   Inconsistent or unrecognized coordinate axis types.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid parameter value.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   x- and y-coordinate arrays are not the same size.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Ill-conditioned coordinate transformation parameters.

        Notes

        The order of the axes for the input world array is determined by
        the `CTYPEia` keywords in the FITS header, therefore it may
        not always be of the form (*ra*, *dec*). The
        [`lat`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat"), [`lng`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng"),
        [`lattyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp"), and
        [`lngtyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp")
        members can be used to determine the order of the axes.

        Using the method of fixed-point iterations approximations we
        iterate starting with the initial approximation, which is
        computed using the non-distortion-aware
        [`wcs_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.wcs_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.wcs_world2pix") (or equivalent).

        The [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") function uses a vectorized
        implementation of the method of consecutive approximations and
        therefore it is highly efficient (>30x) when *all* data points
        that need to be converted from sky coordinates to image
        coordinates are passed at *once*. Therefore, it is advisable,
        whenever possible, to pass as input a long array of all points
        that need to be converted to [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") instead
        of calling [`all_world2pix()`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_world2pix") for each data point. Also
        see the note to the `adaptive` parameter.

        Examples

        ```
        >>> import astropy.io.fits as fits
        >>> import astropy.wcs as wcs
        >>> import numpy as np
        >>> import os

        ```

        ```
        >>> filename = os.path.join(wcs.__path__[0], 'tests/data/j94f05bgq_flt.fits')
        >>> hdulist = fits.open(filename)
        >>> w = wcs.WCS(hdulist[('sci',1)].header, hdulist)
        >>> hdulist.close()

        ```

        ```
        >>> ra, dec = w.all_pix2world([1,2,3], [1,1,1], 1)
        >>> print(ra)
        [ 5.52645627  5.52649663  5.52653698]
        >>> print(dec)
        [-72.05171757 -72.05171276 -72.05170795]
        >>> radec = w.all_pix2world([[1,1], [2,1], [3,1]], 1)
        >>> print(radec)
        [[  5.52645627 -72.05171757]
         [  5.52649663 -72.05171276]
         [  5.52653698 -72.05170795]]
        >>> x, y = w.all_world2pix(ra, dec, 1)
        >>> print(x)
        [ 1.00000238  2.00000237  3.00000236]
        >>> print(y)
        [ 0.99999996  0.99999997  0.99999997]
        >>> xy = w.all_world2pix(radec, 1)
        >>> print(xy)
        [[ 1.00000238  0.99999996]
         [ 2.00000237  0.99999997]
         [ 3.00000236  0.99999997]]
        >>> xy = w.all_world2pix(radec, 1, maxiter=3,
        ...                      tolerance=1.0e-10, quiet=False)
        Traceback (most recent call last):
        ...
        NoConvergence: 'WCS.all_world2pix' failed to converge to the
        requested accuracy. After 3 iterations, the solution is
        diverging at least for one input point.

        ```

        ```
        >>> # Now try to use some diverging data:
        >>> divradec = w.all_pix2world([[1.0, 1.0],
        ...                             [10000.0, 50000.0],
        ...                             [3.0, 1.0]], 1)
        >>> print(divradec)
        [[  5.52645627 -72.05171757]
         [  7.15976932 -70.8140779 ]
         [  5.52653698 -72.05170795]]

        ```

        ```
        >>> # First, turn detect_divergence on:
        >>> try:
        ...   xy = w.all_world2pix(divradec, 1, maxiter=20,
        ...                        tolerance=1.0e-4, adaptive=False,
        ...                        detect_divergence=True,
        ...                        quiet=False)
        ... except wcs.wcs.NoConvergence as e:
        ...   print("Indices of diverging points: {0}"
        ...         .format(e.divergent))
        ...   print("Indices of poorly converging points: {0}"
        ...         .format(e.slow_conv))
        ...   print("Best solution:\n{0}".format(e.best_solution))
        ...   print("Achieved accuracy:\n{0}".format(e.accuracy))
        Indices of diverging points: [1]
        Indices of poorly converging points: None
        Best solution:
        [[  1.00000238e+00   9.99999965e-01]
         [ -1.99441636e+06   1.44309097e+06]
         [  3.00000236e+00   9.99999966e-01]]
        Achieved accuracy:
        [[  6.13968380e-05   8.59638593e-07]
         [  8.59526812e+11   6.61713548e+11]
         [  6.09398446e-05   8.38759724e-07]]
        >>> raise e
        Traceback (most recent call last):
        ...
        NoConvergence: 'WCS.all_world2pix' failed to converge to the
        requested accuracy.  After 5 iterations, the solution is
        diverging at least for one input point.

        ```

        ```
        >>> # This time turn detect_divergence off:
        >>> try:
        ...   xy = w.all_world2pix(divradec, 1, maxiter=20,
        ...                        tolerance=1.0e-4, adaptive=False,
        ...                        detect_divergence=False,
        ...                        quiet=False)
        ... except wcs.wcs.NoConvergence as e:
        ...   print("Indices of diverging points: {0}"
        ...         .format(e.divergent))
        ...   print("Indices of poorly converging points: {0}"
        ...         .format(e.slow_conv))
        ...   print("Best solution:\n{0}".format(e.best_solution))
        ...   print("Achieved accuracy:\n{0}".format(e.accuracy))
        Indices of diverging points: [1]
        Indices of poorly converging points: None
        Best solution:
        [[ 1.00000009  1.        ]
         [        nan         nan]
         [ 3.00000009  1.        ]]
        Achieved accuracy:
        [[  2.29417358e-06   3.21222995e-08]
         [             nan              nan]
         [  2.27407877e-06   3.13005639e-08]]
        >>> raise e
        Traceback (most recent call last):
        ...
        NoConvergence: 'WCS.all_world2pix' failed to converge to the
        requested accuracy.  After 6 iterations, the solution is
        diverging at least for one input point.

        ```

    array\_index\_to\_world(*\*index\_arrays*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.array_index_to_world "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert array indices to world coordinates (represented by Astropy
        objects).

        If a single high-level object is used to represent the world coordinates
        (i.e., if `len(wcs.world_axis_object_classes) == 1`), it is returned
        as-is (not in a tuple/list), otherwise a tuple of high-level objects is
        returned. See
        [`array_index_to_world_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.array_index_to_world_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.array_index_to_world_values") for
        pixel indexing and ordering conventions.

    array\_index\_to\_world\_values(*\*index\_arrays*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.array_index_to_world_values "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert array indices to world coordinates.

        This is the same as [`pixel_to_world_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values") except that
        the indices should be given in `(i, j)` order, where for an image
        `i` is the row and `j` is the column (i.e. the opposite order to
        [`pixel_to_world_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values")).

        If [`world_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim") is `1`, this
        method returns a single scalar or array, otherwise a tuple of scalars or
        arrays is returned.

    calc\_footprint(*header=None*, *undistort=True*, *axes=None*, *center=True*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.calc_footprint)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.calc_footprint "Link to this definition")
    :   Calculates the footprint of the image on the sky.

        A footprint is defined as the positions of the corners of the
        image on the sky after all available distortions have been
        applied.

        Parameters:
        :   **header**[`Header`](../io/fits/api/headers.html#astropy.io.fits.Header "astropy.io.fits.Header") [`object`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#object "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Used to get `NAXIS1` and `NAXIS2`
                header and axes are mutually exclusive, alternative ways
                to provide the same information.

            **undistort**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   If [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"), take SIP and distortion lookup table into
                account

            **axes**([`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)"), [`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)")), optional
            :   If provided, use the given sequence as the shape of the
                image. Otherwise, use the `NAXIS1` and `NAXIS2`
                keywords from the header that was used to create this
                [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") object.

            **center**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   If [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)") use the center of the pixel, otherwise use the corner.

        Returns:
        :   **coord**(4, 2) [`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)") of (*x*, *y*) coordinates.
            :   The order is clockwise starting with the bottom left corner.

    copy()[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.copy)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.copy "Link to this definition")
    :   Return a shallow copy of the object.

        Convenience method so user doesn’t have to import the
        [`copy`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/copy.html#module-copy "(in Python v3.15)") stdlib module.

        Warning

        Use [`deepcopy`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.deepcopy "astropy.wcs.WCS.deepcopy") instead of [`copy`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.copy "astropy.wcs.WCS.copy") unless you know why you need a
        shallow copy.

    deepcopy()[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.deepcopy)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.deepcopy "Link to this definition")
    :   Return a deep copy of the object.

        Convenience method so user doesn’t have to import the
        [`copy`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/copy.html#module-copy "(in Python v3.15)") stdlib module.

    det2im(*\*args*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.det2im)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.det2im "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert detector coordinates to image plane coordinates using
        [distortion paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table-lookup correction.

        The output is in absolute pixel coordinates, not relative to
        `CRPIX`.

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *2* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the pixel coordinates. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

    dropaxis(*dropax*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.dropaxis)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.dropaxis "Link to this definition")
    :   Remove an axis from the WCS.

        Parameters:
        :   **dropax**[`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   The index of the WCS to drop, counting from 0 (i.e., python convention,
                not FITS convention)

        Returns:
        :   [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS")
            :   A new [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") instance with one axis fewer

    fix(*translate\_units=''*, *naxis=None*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.fix)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.fix "Link to this definition")
    :   Perform the fix operations from wcslib, and warn about any
        changes it has made.

        Parameters:
        :   **translate\_units**[`str`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Specify which potentially unsafe translations of
                non-standard unit strings to perform. By default,
                performs none.

                Although `"S"` is commonly used to represent seconds,
                its translation to `"s"` is potentially unsafe since the
                standard recognizes `"S"` formally as Siemens, however
                rarely that may be used. The same applies to `"H"` for
                hours (Henry), and `"D"` for days (Debye).

                This string controls what to do in such cases, and is
                case-insensitive.

                * If the string contains `"s"`, translate `"S"` to
                  `"s"`.
                * If the string contains `"h"`, translate `"H"` to
                  `"h"`.
                * If the string contains `"d"`, translate `"D"` to
                  `"d"`.

                Thus `''` doesn’t do any unsafe translations, whereas
                `'shd'` does all of them.

            **naxis**[`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)") [`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)"), optional
            :   Image axis lengths. If this array is set to zero or
                `None`, then [`cylfix`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.cylfix "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.cylfix") will not be
                invoked.

    footprint\_contains(*coord*, *\*\*kwargs*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.footprint_contains)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.footprint_contains "Link to this definition")
    :   Determines if a given SkyCoord is contained in the wcs footprint.

        Parameters:
        :   **coord**[`SkyCoord`](astropy.coordinates.SkyCoord.html#astropy.coordinates.SkyCoord "astropy.coordinates.SkyCoord")
            :   The coordinate to check if it is within the wcs coordinate.

            **\*\*kwargs**
            :   Additional arguments to pass to [`to_pixel`](astropy.coordinates.SkyCoord.html#astropy.coordinates.SkyCoord.to_pixel "astropy.coordinates.SkyCoord.to_pixel")

        Returns:
        :   **response**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   True means the WCS footprint contains the coordinate, False means it does not.

    footprint\_to\_file(*filename='footprint.reg'*, *color='green'*, *width=2*, *coordsys=None*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.footprint_to_file)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.footprint_to_file "Link to this definition")
    :   Writes out a [ds9](http://ds9.si.edu/) style regions file. It can be loaded
        directly by [ds9](http://ds9.si.edu/).

        Parameters:
        :   **filename**[`str`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Output file name - default is `'footprint.reg'`

            **color**[`str`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Color to use when plotting the line.

            **width**[`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Width of the region line.

            **coordsys**[`str`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Coordinate system. If not specified (default), the `radesys`
                value is used. For all possible values, see
                <http://ds9.si.edu/doc/ref/region.html#RegionFileFormat>

    get\_axis\_types()[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.get_axis_types)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.get_axis_types "Link to this definition")
    :   Similar to [`self.wcsprm.axis_types`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.axis_types "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.axis_types")
        but provides the information in a more Python-friendly format.

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`list`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#list "(in Python v3.15)") of [`dict`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#dict "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Returns a list of dictionaries, one for each axis, each
                containing attributes about the type of that axis.

                Each dictionary has the following keys:

                * ‘coordinate\_type’:

                  + None: Non-specific coordinate type.
                  + ‘stokes’: Stokes coordinate.
                  + ‘celestial’: Celestial coordinate (including `CUBEFACE`).
                  + ‘spectral’: Spectral coordinate.
                * ‘scale’:

                  + ‘linear’: Linear axis.
                  + ‘quantized’: Quantized axis (`STOKES`, `CUBEFACE`).
                  + ‘non-linear celestial’: Non-linear celestial axis.
                  + ‘non-linear spectral’: Non-linear spectral axis.
                  + ‘logarithmic’: Logarithmic axis.
                  + ‘tabular’: Tabular axis.
                * ‘group’

                  + Group number, e.g. lookup table number
                * ‘number’

                  + For celestial axes:

                    - 0: Longitude coordinate.
                    - 1: Latitude coordinate.
                    - 2: `CUBEFACE` number.
                  + For lookup tables:

                    - the axis number in a multidimensional table.

                `CTYPEia` in `"4-3"` form with unrecognized algorithm code will
                generate an error.

    p4\_pix2foc(*\*args*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.p4_pix2foc)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.p4_pix2foc "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert pixel coordinates to focal plane coordinates using
        [distortion paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table-lookup correction.

        The output is in absolute pixel coordinates, not relative to
        `CRPIX`.

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *2* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the focal coordinates. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

    pix2foc(*\*args*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.pix2foc)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.pix2foc "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert pixel coordinates to focal plane coordinates using the
        [SIP](https://irsa.ipac.caltech.edu/data/SPITZER/docs/files/spitzer/shupeADASS.pdf) polynomial distortion convention and [distortion
        paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table-lookup correction.

        The output is in absolute pixel coordinates, not relative to
        `CRPIX`.

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *2* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the focal coordinates. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

    pixel\_to\_world(*\*pixel\_arrays*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.pixel_to_world "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert pixel coordinates to world coordinates (represented by
        high-level objects).

        If a single high-level object is used to represent the world coordinates
        (i.e., if `len(wcs.world_axis_object_classes) == 1`), it is returned
        as-is (not in a tuple/list), otherwise a tuple of high-level objects is
        returned. See
        [`pixel_to_world_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values") for pixel
        indexing and ordering conventions.

    pixel\_to\_world\_values(*\*pixel\_arrays*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.pixel_to_world_values "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert pixel coordinates to world coordinates.

        This method takes [`pixel_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim") scalars or arrays as
        input, and pixel coordinates should be zero-based. Returns
        [`world_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim") scalars or arrays in units given by
        [`world_axis_units`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_axis_units "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_axis_units"). Note that pixel coordinates are
        assumed to be 0 at the center of the first pixel in each dimension. If a
        pixel is in a region where the WCS is not defined, NaN should be returned.
        The coordinates should be specified in the `(x, y)` order, where for
        an image, `x` is the horizontal coordinate and `y` is the vertical
        coordinate.

        If [`world_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim") is `1`, this
        method returns a single scalar or array, otherwise a tuple of scalars or
        arrays is returned.

    printwcs()[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.printwcs)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.printwcs "Link to this definition")

    proj\_plane\_pixel\_area()[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.proj_plane_pixel_area)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.proj_plane_pixel_area "Link to this definition")
    :   For a **celestial** WCS (see [`astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial "astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial")), returns pixel
        area of the image pixel at the `CRPIX` location once it is projected
        onto the “plane of intermediate world coordinates” as defined in
        [Greisen & Calabretta 2002, A&A, 395, 1061](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2002A%26A...395.1061G).

        Note

        This function is concerned **only** about the transformation
        “image plane”->”projection plane” and **not** about the
        transformation “celestial sphere”->”projection plane”->”image plane”.
        Therefore, this function ignores distortions arising due to
        non-linear nature of most projections.

        Note

        This method only returns sensible answers if the WCS contains
        celestial axes, i.e., the [`celestial`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial "astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial") WCS object.

        Returns:
        :   **area**[`Quantity`](astropy.units.Quantity.html#astropy.units.Quantity "astropy.units.Quantity")
            :   Area (in the projection plane) of the pixel at `CRPIX` location.

        Raises:
        :   [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Pixel area is defined only for 2D pixels. Most likely the
                [`cd`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.cd "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.cd") matrix of the [`celestial`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial "astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial")
                WCS is not a square matrix of second order.

        See also

        [`astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_area`](astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_area.html#astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_area "astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_area")

        Notes

        Depending on the application, square root of the pixel area can be used to
        represent a single pixel scale of an equivalent square pixel
        whose area is equal to the area of a generally non-square pixel.

    proj\_plane\_pixel\_scales()[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.proj_plane_pixel_scales)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.proj_plane_pixel_scales "Link to this definition")
    :   Calculate pixel scales along each axis of the image pixel at
        the `CRPIX` location once it is projected onto the
        “plane of intermediate world coordinates” as defined in
        [Greisen & Calabretta 2002, A&A, 395, 1061](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2002A%26A...395.1061G).

        Note

        This method is concerned **only** about the transformation
        “image plane”->”projection plane” and **not** about the
        transformation “celestial sphere”->”projection plane”->”image plane”.
        Therefore, this function ignores distortions arising due to
        non-linear nature of most projections.

        Note

        This method only returns sensible answers if the WCS contains
        celestial axes, i.e., the [`celestial`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial "astropy.wcs.WCS.celestial") WCS object.

        Returns:
        :   **scale**[`list`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#list "(in Python v3.15)") of [`Quantity`](astropy.units.Quantity.html#astropy.units.Quantity "astropy.units.Quantity")
            :   A vector of projection plane increments corresponding to each
                pixel side (axis).

        See also

        [`astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_scales`](astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_scales.html#astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_scales "astropy.wcs.utils.proj_plane_pixel_scales")

    reorient\_celestial\_first()[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.reorient_celestial_first)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.reorient_celestial_first "Link to this definition")
    :   Reorient the WCS such that the celestial axes are first, followed by
        the spectral axis, followed by any others.
        Assumes at least celestial axes are present.

    sip\_foc2pix(*\*args*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.sip_foc2pix)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.sip_foc2pix "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert focal plane coordinates to pixel coordinates using the
        [SIP](https://irsa.ipac.caltech.edu/data/SPITZER/docs/files/spitzer/shupeADASS.pdf) polynomial distortion convention.

        FITS WCS [distortion paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table lookup distortion
        correction is not applied, even if that information existed in
        the FITS file that initialized this [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") object.

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *2* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the pixel coordinates. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

    sip\_pix2foc(*\*args*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.sip_pix2foc)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.sip_pix2foc "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert pixel coordinates to focal plane coordinates using the
        [SIP](https://irsa.ipac.caltech.edu/data/SPITZER/docs/files/spitzer/shupeADASS.pdf) polynomial distortion convention.

        The output is in pixel coordinates, relative to `CRPIX`.

        FITS WCS [distortion paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table lookup correction is not
        applied, even if that information existed in the FITS file
        that initialized this [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") object. To
        correct for that, use [`pix2foc`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.pix2foc "astropy.wcs.WCS.pix2foc") or
        [`p4_pix2foc`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.p4_pix2foc "astropy.wcs.WCS.p4_pix2foc").

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *2* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the focal coordinates. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

    slice(*view*, *numpy\_order=True*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.slice)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.slice "Link to this definition")
    :   Slice a WCS instance using a Numpy slice. The order of the slice should
        be reversed (as for the data) compared to the natural WCS order.

        Parameters:
        :   **view**[`tuple`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#tuple "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   A tuple containing the same number of slices as the WCS system.
                If the shape of the data is known (for example if `array_shape`
                is set), negative values for the `start` and `stop` attributes
                of the slices are interpreted relative to the end of the axis, as
                for Numpy arrays. If the shape is not known, a negative `start`
                raises a [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)"), since the offset of the sliced WCS relative
                to the original one cannot be determined. A negative `stop` is
                still allowed in this case, since it only determines the number of
                pixels along the sliced axis, which is unknown anyway, and does not
                affect the coordinate transformation.
                The `step` attribute of the slices can be [`None`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#None "(in Python v3.15)") or a positive
                integer, in which case the pixel grid is downsampled by that
                factor. Negative steps, which would reverse an axis, are not
                supported.

            **numpy\_order**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), default: [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Use numpy order, i.e. slice the WCS so that an identical slice
                applied to a numpy array will slice the array and WCS in the same
                way. If set to [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)"), the WCS will be sliced in FITS order,
                meaning the first slice will be applied to the *last* numpy index
                but the *first* WCS axis.

        Returns:
        :   **wcs\_new**[`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS")
            :   A new resampled WCS axis

    sub(*axes*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.sub)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.sub "Link to this definition")
    :   Extracts the coordinate description for a subimage from a
        [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") object.

        The world coordinate system of the subimage must be separable in the
        sense that the world coordinates at any point in the subimage must
        depend only on the pixel coordinates of the axes extracted. In
        practice, this means that the `PCi_ja` matrix of the original image
        must not contain non-zero off-diagonal terms that associate any of the
        subimage axes with any of the non-subimage axes.

        [`sub`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.sub "astropy.wcs.WCS.sub") can also add axes to a wcsprm object. The new axes will be
        created using the defaults set by the Wcsprm constructor which produce
        a simple, unnamed, linear axis with world coordinates equal to the
        pixel coordinate. These default values can be changed before
        invoking [`set`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#set "(in Python v3.15)").

        Parameters:
        :   **axes**[`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)") or `a` sequence.
            :   * If an int, include the first *N* axes in their original order.
                * If a sequence, may contain a combination of image axis numbers
                  (1-relative) or special axis identifiers (see below). Order is
                  significant; `axes[0]` is the axis number of the input image
                  that corresponds to the first axis in the subimage, etc. Use an
                  axis number of 0 to create a new axis using the defaults.
                * If `0`, `[]` or `None`, do a deep copy.

                Coordinate axes types may be specified using either strings or
                special integer constants. The available types are:

                * `'longitude'` / `WCSSUB_LONGITUDE`: Celestial longitude
                * `'latitude'` / `WCSSUB_LATITUDE`: Celestial latitude
                * `'cubeface'` / `WCSSUB_CUBEFACE`: Quadcube `CUBEFACE` axis
                * `'spectral'` / `WCSSUB_SPECTRAL`: Spectral axis
                * `'stokes'` / `WCSSUB_STOKES`: Stokes axis
                * `'temporal'` / `WCSSUB_TIME`: Time axis (requires `WCSLIB` version 7.8 or greater)
                * `'celestial'` / `WCSSUB_CELESTIAL`: An alias for the
                  combination of `'longitude'`, `'latitude'` and `'cubeface'`.

        Returns:
        :   **new\_wcs**[`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") [`object`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#object "(in Python v3.15)")

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`InvalidSubimageSpecificationError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidSubimageSpecificationError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidSubimageSpecificationError "astropy.wcs.InvalidSubimageSpecificationError")
            :   Invalid subimage specification (no spectral axis).

            [`NonseparableSubimageCoordinateSystemError`](astropy.wcs.NonseparableSubimageCoordinateSystemError.html#astropy.wcs.NonseparableSubimageCoordinateSystemError "astropy.wcs.NonseparableSubimageCoordinateSystemError")
            :   Non-separable subimage coordinate system.

        Notes

        Combinations of subimage axes of particular types may be extracted in
        the same order as they occur in the input image by combining the
        integer constants with the ‘binary or’ (`|`) operator. For
        example:

        ```
        wcs.sub([WCSSUB_LONGITUDE | WCSSUB_LATITUDE | WCSSUB_SPECTRAL])

        ```

        would extract the longitude, latitude, and spectral axes in the same
        order as the input image. If one of each were present, the resulting
        object would have three dimensions.

        For convenience, `WCSSUB_CELESTIAL` is defined as the combination
        `WCSSUB_LONGITUDE | WCSSUB_LATITUDE | WCSSUB_CUBEFACE`.

        The codes may also be negated to extract all but the types specified,
        for example:

        ```
        wcs.sub([
          WCSSUB_LONGITUDE,
          WCSSUB_LATITUDE,
          WCSSUB_CUBEFACE,
          -(WCSSUB_SPECTRAL | WCSSUB_STOKES)])

        ```

        The last of these specifies all axis types other than spectral or
        Stokes. Extraction is done in the order specified by `axes`, i.e. a
        longitude axis (if present) would be extracted first (via `axes[0]`)
        and not subsequently (via `axes[3]`). Likewise for the latitude and
        cubeface axes in this example.

        The number of dimensions in the returned object may be less than or
        greater than the length of `axes`. However, it will never exceed the
        number of axes in the input image.

    swapaxes(*ax0*, *ax1*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.swapaxes)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.swapaxes "Link to this definition")
    :   Swap axes in a WCS.

        Parameters:
        :   **ax0**[`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)")


            **ax1**[`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   The indices of the WCS to be swapped, counting from 0 (i.e., python
                convention, not FITS convention)

        Returns:
        :   [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS")
            :   A new [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") instance with the same number of axes,
                but two swapped

    to\_fits(*relax=False*, *key=None*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.to_fits)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.to_fits "Link to this definition")
    :   Generate an [`HDUList`](../io/fits/api/hdulists.html#astropy.io.fits.HDUList "astropy.io.fits.HDUList") object with all of the
        information stored in this object. This should be logically identical
        to the input FITS file, but it will be normalized in a number of ways.

        See [`to_header`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header "astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header") for some warnings about the output produced.

        Parameters:
        :   **relax**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)") or [`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Degree of permissiveness:

                * [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)") (default): Write all extensions that are
                  considered to be safe and recommended.
                * [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"): Write all recognized informal extensions of the
                  WCS standard.
                * [`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)"): a bit field selecting specific extensions to
                  write. See [Header-writing relaxation constants](../wcs/relax.html#relaxwrite) for details.

            **key**[`str`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   The name of a particular WCS transform to use. This may be
                either `' '` or `'A'`-`'Z'` and corresponds to the `"a"`
                part of the `CTYPEia` cards.

        Returns:
        :   **hdulist**[`HDUList`](../io/fits/api/hdulists.html#astropy.io.fits.HDUList "astropy.io.fits.HDUList")

    to\_header(*relax=None*, *key=None*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.to_header)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header "Link to this definition")
    :   Generate an [`astropy.io.fits.Header`](../io/fits/api/headers.html#astropy.io.fits.Header "astropy.io.fits.Header") object with the basic WCS
        and SIP information stored in this object. This should be
        logically identical to the input FITS file, but it will be
        normalized in a number of ways.

        Warning

        This function does not write out FITS WCS [distortion
        paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) information, since that requires multiple FITS
        header data units. To get a full representation of
        everything in this object, use [`to_fits`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.to_fits "astropy.wcs.WCS.to_fits").

        Parameters:
        :   **relax**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)") or [`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   Degree of permissiveness:

                * [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)") (default): Write all extensions that are
                  considered to be safe and recommended.
                * [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)"): Write all recognized informal extensions of the
                  WCS standard.
                * [`int`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/functions.html#int "(in Python v3.15)"): a bit field selecting specific extensions to
                  write. See [Header-writing relaxation constants](../wcs/relax.html#relaxwrite) for details.

                If the `relax` keyword argument is not given and any
                keywords were omitted from the output, an
                [`AstropyWarning`](astropy.utils.exceptions.AstropyWarning.html#astropy.utils.exceptions.AstropyWarning "astropy.utils.exceptions.AstropyWarning") is displayed.
                To override this, explicitly pass a value to `relax`.

            **key**[`str`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#str "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   The name of a particular WCS transform to use. This may be
                either `' '` or `'A'`-`'Z'` and corresponds to the `"a"`
                part of the `CTYPEia` cards.

        Returns:
        :   **header**[`astropy.io.fits.Header`](../io/fits/api/headers.html#astropy.io.fits.Header "astropy.io.fits.Header")

        Notes

        The output header will almost certainly differ from the input in a
        number of respects:

        > 1. The output header only contains WCS-related keywords. In
        >    particular, it does not contain syntactically-required
        >    keywords such as `SIMPLE`, `NAXIS`, `BITPIX`, or
        >    `END`.
        > 2. Deprecated (e.g. `CROTAn`) or non-standard usage will
        >    be translated to standard (this is partially dependent on
        >    whether `fix` was applied).
        > 3. Unless the [`WCS`](#astropy.wcs.WCS "astropy.wcs.WCS") was created with `preserve_units=True`,
        >    quantities will be converted to the units used internally,
        >    basically SI with the addition of degrees. If
        >    `preserve_units=True`, quantities are written in the
        >    original units given by `CUNITia`.
        > 4. Floating-point quantities may be given to a different decimal
        >    precision.
        > 5. Elements of the `PCi_j` matrix will be written if and
        >    only if they differ from the unit matrix. Thus, if the
        >    matrix is unity then no elements will be written.
        > 6. Additional keywords such as `WCSAXES`, `CUNITia`,
        >    `LONPOLEa` and `LATPOLEa` may appear.
        > 7. The original keycomments will be lost, although
        >    [`to_header`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header "astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header") tries hard to write meaningful comments.
        > 8. Keyword order may be changed.

    to\_header\_string(*relax=None*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.to_header_string)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header_string "Link to this definition")
    :   Identical to [`to_header`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header "astropy.wcs.WCS.to_header"), but returns a string containing the
        header cards.

    wcs\_pix2world(*\*args*, *\*\*kwargs*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.wcs_pix2world)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.wcs_pix2world "Link to this definition")
    :   Transforms pixel coordinates to world coordinates by doing
        only the basic [wcslib](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/wcslib/index.html) transformation.

        No [SIP](https://irsa.ipac.caltech.edu/data/SPITZER/docs/files/spitzer/shupeADASS.pdf) or [distortion paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table lookup correction is
        applied. To perform distortion correction, see
        [`all_pix2world`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.all_pix2world "astropy.wcs.WCS.all_pix2world"),
        [`sip_pix2foc`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.sip_pix2foc "astropy.wcs.WCS.sip_pix2foc"), [`p4_pix2foc`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.p4_pix2foc "astropy.wcs.WCS.p4_pix2foc"),
        or [`pix2foc`](#astropy.wcs.WCS.pix2foc "astropy.wcs.WCS.pix2foc").

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *naxis* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

                For a transformation that is not two-dimensional, the
                two-argument form must be used.

            **ra\_dec\_order**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   When [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)") will ensure that world coordinates are always given
                and returned in as (*ra*, *dec*) pairs, regardless of the order of
                the axes specified by the in the `CTYPE` keywords. Default is
                [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)").

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the world coordinates, in degrees. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`SingularMatrixError`](astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError.html#astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError "astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError")
            :   Linear transformation matrix is singular.

            [`InconsistentAxisTypesError`](astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError.html#astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError "astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError")
            :   Inconsistent or unrecognized coordinate axis types.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid parameter value.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   x- and y-coordinate arrays are not the same size.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Ill-conditioned coordinate transformation parameters.

        Notes

        The order of the axes for the result is determined by the
        `CTYPEia` keywords in the FITS header, therefore it may not
        always be of the form (*ra*, *dec*). The
        [`lat`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat"), [`lng`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng"),
        [`lattyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp") and [`lngtyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp")
        members can be used to determine the order of the axes.

    wcs\_world2pix(*\*args*, *\*\*kwargs*)[[source]](../_modules/astropy/wcs/wcs.html#WCS.wcs_world2pix)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.wcs_world2pix "Link to this definition")
    :   Transforms world coordinates to pixel coordinates, using only
        the basic [wcslib](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/wcslib/index.html) WCS transformation. No [SIP](https://irsa.ipac.caltech.edu/data/SPITZER/docs/files/spitzer/shupeADASS.pdf) or
        [distortion paper](https://www.atnf.csiro.au/people/mcalabre/WCS/dcs_20040422.pdf) table lookup transformation is applied.

        Parameters:
        :   **\*args**
            :   There are two accepted forms for the positional arguments:

                > * 2 arguments: An *N* x *naxis* array of coordinates, and an
                >   *origin*.
                > * more than 2 arguments: An array for each axis, followed by
                >   an *origin*. These arrays must be broadcastable to one
                >   another.

                Here, *origin* is the coordinate in the upper left corner of the
                image. In FITS and Fortran standards, this is 1. In Numpy and C
                standards this is 0.

                For a transformation that is not two-dimensional, the
                two-argument form must be used.

            **ra\_dec\_order**[bool](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/stdtypes.html#bltin-boolean-values "(in Python v3.15)"), optional
            :   When [`True`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#True "(in Python v3.15)") will ensure that world coordinates are always given
                and returned in as (*ra*, *dec*) pairs, regardless of the order of
                the axes specified by the in the `CTYPE` keywords. Default is
                [`False`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/constants.html#False "(in Python v3.15)").

        Returns:
        :   **result**[`array`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.ndarray.html#numpy.ndarray "(in NumPy v2.5)")
            :   Returns the pixel coordinates. If the input was a single array and
                origin, a single array is returned, otherwise a tuple of arrays is
                returned.

        Raises:
        :   [`MemoryError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#MemoryError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Memory allocation failed.

            [`SingularMatrixError`](astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError.html#astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError "astropy.wcs.SingularMatrixError")
            :   Linear transformation matrix is singular.

            [`InconsistentAxisTypesError`](astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError.html#astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError "astropy.wcs.InconsistentAxisTypesError")
            :   Inconsistent or unrecognized coordinate axis types.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid parameter value.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`ValueError`](https://docs.python.org/3/builtins/exceptions.html#ValueError "(in Python v3.15)")
            :   x- and y-coordinate arrays are not the same size.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Invalid coordinate transformation parameters.

            [`InvalidTransformError`](astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError.html#astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError "astropy.wcs.InvalidTransformError")
            :   Ill-conditioned coordinate transformation parameters.

        Notes

        The order of the axes for the input world array is determined by
        the `CTYPEia` keywords in the FITS header, therefore it may
        not always be of the form (*ra*, *dec*). The
        [`lat`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lat"), [`lng`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lng"),
        [`lattyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lattyp") and [`lngtyp`](astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.html#astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp "astropy.wcs.Wcsprm.lngtyp")
        members can be used to determine the order of the axes.

    world\_to\_array\_index(*\*world\_objects*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.world_to_array_index "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert world coordinates (represented by Astropy objects) to array
        indices.

        If [`pixel_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim") is `1`, this
        method returns a single scalar or array, otherwise a tuple of scalars or
        arrays is returned. See
        [`world_to_array_index_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_to_array_index_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_to_array_index_values") for
        pixel indexing and ordering conventions. The indices should be returned
        as rounded integers.

    world\_to\_array\_index\_values(*\*world\_arrays*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.world_to_array_index_values "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert world coordinates to array indices.

        This is the same as [`world_to_pixel_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_to_pixel_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_to_pixel_values") except that
        the indices should be returned in `(i, j)` order, where for an image
        `i` is the row and `j` is the column (i.e. the opposite order to
        [`pixel_to_world_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_to_world_values")). The indices should be
        returned as rounded integers.

        If [`pixel_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim") is `1`, this
        method returns a single scalar or array, otherwise a tuple of scalars or
        arrays is returned.

    world\_to\_pixel(*\*world\_objects*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.world_to_pixel "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert world coordinates (represented by Astropy objects) to pixel
        coordinates.

        If [`pixel_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim") is `1`, this
        method returns a single scalar or array, otherwise a tuple of scalars or
        arrays is returned. See
        [`world_to_pixel_values`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_to_pixel_values "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_to_pixel_values") for pixel
        indexing and ordering conventions.

    world\_to\_pixel\_values(*\*world\_arrays*)[#](#astropy.wcs.WCS.world_to_pixel_values "Link to this definition")
    :   Convert world coordinates to pixel coordinates.

        This method takes [`world_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_n_dim") scalars or arrays as
        input in units given by [`world_axis_units`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_axis_units "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.world_axis_units"). Returns
        [`pixel_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim") scalars or arrays. Note that pixel
        coordinates are assumed to be 0 at the center of the first pixel in each
        dimension. If a world coordinate does not have a matching pixel
        coordinate, NaN should be returned. The coordinates should be returned in
        the `(x, y)` order, where for an image, `x` is the horizontal
        coordinate and `y` is the vertical coordinate.

        If [`pixel_n_dim`](astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.html#astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim "astropy.wcs.wcsapi.BaseLowLevelWCS.pixel_n_dim") is `1`, this
        method returns a single scalar or array, otherwise a tuple of scalars or
        arrays is returned.
